We are given supply and demand functions. Supply: P = ln(Q_S^3 - 2). Demand: P = 18 e^{-arctan(Q_D)}. Find total welfare using market equilibrium condition. Need market equilibrium quantity Q* where Q_S = Q_D = Q. Then find equilibrium price P*. Then total welfare = consumer surplus + producer surplus. Consumer surplus is area under demand curve minus price times quantity? Actually consumer surplus = ∫_0^{Q*} (Demand function) dQ - P* Q*. Producer surplus = P* Q* - ∫_0^{Q*} (Supply function) dQ? But careful: supply function is given as P = ln(Q^3 - 2). That function is defined only for Q such that Q^3 - 2 > 0, i.e., Q > cube root of 2 ≈ 1.26. So supply curve starts at Q > 2^{1/3}. For Q below that, supply is not defined; presumably supply is zero for Q ≤ that? But typical supply function might be defined as P = ln(Q^3 - 2) for Q > 2^{1/3}, and for lower Q, we can treat it as not producing. But equilibrium Q might be above that. We need to solve equilibrium.

Set ln(Q^3 - 2) = 18 e^{-arctan(Q)}. Let Q be the equilibrium quantity. Solve numerically.

Let's try differentiating. Suppose F(x) = (ax+b) e^{-arctan x}. Then F'(x) = a e^{-arctan x} + (ax+b) e^{-arctan x} * (-1/(1+x^2)) = e^{-arctan x} [a - (ax+b)/(1+x^2)]. Maybe F(x) = (ax^2+bx+c) e^{-arctan x}? Let's try: F' = (2ax+b) e^{-arctan x} + (ax^2+bx+c) e^{-arctan x} * (-1/(1+x^2)) = e^{-arctan x}[2ax+b - (ax^2+bx+c)/(1+x^2)]. Set equal to 18. So we need:

x^2: b - a = 18 => b = 18.
x^1: 2a - b = 0 => -b=0 => b=0, contradiction. So no polynomial times e^{-arctan} works because right side is constant plus x^2 term, but left side has x^3, x^2, x, constant. Now we need ∫_0^{4.68985} D(Q) dQ. We'll compute using Simpson's rule for 0 to 4.6 with step 0.2, and then add the last segment from 4.6 to 4.68985. For 0 to 4.6, we have points at Q=0,0.2,...,4.6. That's 24 points (0 to 4.6 inclusive, step 0.2). Number of intervals = 23, which is odd. So we cannot apply standard Simpson's 1/3 rule over the whole range because it requires even number of intervals. Alternatively, we can compute the integral using the antiderivative formula with the cancellation handled analytically, which might be more accurate. Let's attempt that route. We have I_s = ∫_a^{Q*} ln(Q^3-2) dQ = [Q ln(Q^3-2) - 3Q]_a^{Q*} - 6 ∫_a^{Q*} dQ/(Q^3-2). The term ∫ dQ/(Q^3-2) can be evaluated using the formula, and the singularities will cancel. Let's compute using that.

Let a = 2^{1/3}. Define F(Q) = Q ln(Q^3-2) - 3Q. Compute at Q=Q* and at Q=a+ (limit). At Q=a, Q^3-2=0, so ln(0) = -∞, but F(Q) also has a term that diverges, but we will combine with the -6G term.

Let G(Q) = ∫ dQ/(Q^3-2) with antiderivative as given. Then I_s = lim_{Q→a+} [F(Q) - 6G(Q)] evaluated from a to Q*? Actually I_s = [F(Q) - 6G(Q)]_{a}^{Q*} = (F(Q*) - 6G(Q*)) - (F(a) - 6G(a)). But as Q→a+, both F and G diverge, but the combination should be finite. So we need to compute the limit.

Let's compute G(Q) explicitly: For a = 2^{1/3} ≈ 1.259921, a^2 = 2^{2/3} ≈ 1.587401, a^3=2. So

G(Q) = 1/(3a^2) ln|Q-a| - 1/(6a^2) ln(Q^2 + aQ + a^2) - 1/(a^2√3) arctan((2Q+a)/(a√3)) + C.

We can choose C=0 for convenience. Now let's define H(Q) = F(Q) - 6G(Q). Then I_s = H(Q*) - lim_{Q→a+} H(Q). So we need to compute H(a+).

Compute H(Q) = Q ln(Q^3-2) - 3Q - 6[1/(3a^2) ln|Q-a| - 1/(6a^2) ln(Q^2+aQ+a^2) - 1/(a^2√3) arctan((2Q+a)/(a√3))].

Simplify: -6 * (1/(3a^2)) ln|Q-a| = - (2/a^2) ln|Q-a|.
-6 * (-1/(6a^2) ln(Q^2+aQ+a^2)) = + (1/a^2) ln(Q^2+aQ+a^2).
-6 * (-1/(a^2√3) arctan(...)) = + (6/(a^2√3)) arctan((2Q+a)/(a√3)).

So H(Q) = Q ln(Q^3-2) - 3Q - (2/a^2) ln|Q-a| + (1/a^2) ln(Q^2+aQ+a^2) + (6/(a^2√3)) arctan((2Q+a)/(a√3)).

Now as Q→a+, Q^3-2 = (Q-a)(Q^2+aQ+a^2). So ln(Q^3-2) = ln|Q-a| + ln(Q^2+aQ+a^2). Then Q ln(Q^3-2) = Q ln|Q-a| + Q ln(Q^2+aQ+a^2). So H(Q) becomes:

H(Q) = Q ln|Q-a| + Q ln(Q^2+aQ+a^2) - 3Q - (2/a^2) ln|Q-a| + (1/a^2) ln(Q^2+aQ+a^2) + (6/(a^2√3)) arctan((2Q+a)/(a√3)).

Combine the ln|Q-a| terms: (Q - 2/a^2) ln|Q-a|. As Q→a, Q→a, so this term becomes (a - 2/a^2) ln|Q-a|. But a = 2^{1/3}, so a^3=2 => a^2 = 2/a? Actually a^3=2 => a = 2^{1/3}, then 2/a^2 = 2 / a^2 = 2 / (2^{2/3}) = 2^{1 - 2/3} = 2^{1/3} = a. So 2/a^2 = a. Therefore a - 2/a^2 = a - a = 0. So the coefficient of ln|Q-a| vanishes! That's the cancellation. So H(Q) has no singularity. Great.

Thus H(Q) = Q ln(Q^2+aQ+a^2) - 3Q + (1/a^2) ln(Q^2+aQ+a^2) + (6/(a^2√3)) arctan((2Q+a)/(a√3)). And as Q→a, we can evaluate the limit by plugging Q=a:

H(a) = a ln(a^2+a*a+a^2) - 3a + (1/a^2) ln(a^2+a*a+a^2) + (6/(a^2√3)) arctan((2a+a)/(a√3)) = a ln(3a^2) - 3a + (1/a^2) ln(3a^2) + (6/(a^2√3)) arctan(3a/(a√3)) = a ln(3a^2) - 3a + (1/a^2) ln(3a^2) + (6/(a^2√3)) arctan(3/√3) = a ln(3a^2) - 3a + (1/a^2) ln(3a^2) + (6/(a^2√3)) arctan(√3). I think I made a mistake in the derivation of I_s. Because I_s = ∫ ln(Q^3-2) dQ. Our antiderivative from integration by parts was: ∫ ln(Q^3-2) dQ = Q ln(Q^3-2) - 3Q - 6∫ dQ/(Q^3-2). Then we used the formula for ∫ dQ/(Q^3-2). But maybe the constant in the antiderivative for ∫ dQ/(Q^3-2) has a sign error? Or the formula might be different for a^3=2? Let's derive the integral of 1/(Q^3 - a^3) properly.

We have ∫ dQ/(Q^3 - a^3) = 1/(3a^2) ln|Q-a| - 1/(6a^2) ln(Q^2+aQ+a^2) - 1/(a^2√3) arctan((2Q+a)/(a√3)) + C. This is standard. Let's verify by differentiating. Let F(Q) = 1/(3a^2) ln(Q-a) - 1/(6a^2) ln(Q^2+aQ+a^2) - 1/(a^2√3) arctan((2Q+a)/(a√3)). Then differentiate:
d/dQ [1/(3a^2) ln(Q-a)] = 1/(3a^2) * 1/(Q-a).
d/dQ [ -1/(6a^2) ln(Q^2+aQ+a^2) ] = -1/(6a^2) * (2Q+a)/(Q^2+aQ+a^2).
d/dQ [ -1/(a^2√3) arctan((2Q+a)/(a√3)) ] = -1/(a^2√3) * 1/(1+((2Q+a)/(a√3))^2) * (2/(a√3)) = -1/(a^2√3) * (1/(1+((2Q+a)^2/(3a^2)))) * (2/(a√3)) = -1/(a^2√3) * (3a^2/(3a^2+(2Q+a)^2)) * (2/(a√3)) = - (3a^2)/(a^2√3) * 1/(3a^2+(2Q+a)^2) * (2/(a√3)) = - (3/√3) * (2/(a√3)) * 1/(3a^2+(2Q+a)^2) = - (3*2)/(a*3) * 1/(3a^2+(2Q+a)^2) because √3*√3=3? Let's do systematically: (3a^2)/(a^2√3)=3/√3=√3. Then times (2/(a√3)) gives (√3)*(2/(a√3)) = 2/a. So derivative = - (2/a) * 1/(3a^2+(2Q+a)^2). Now denominator: 3a^2+(2Q+a)^2 = 3a^2 + 4Q^2+4aQ+a^2 = 4Q^2+4aQ+4a^2 = 4(Q^2+aQ+a^2). So derivative = - (2/a) * 1/(4(Q^2+aQ+a^2)) = -1/(2a(Q^2+aQ+a^2)). So sum of derivatives: 1/(3a^2(Q-a)) - (2Q+a)/(6a^2(Q^2+aQ+a^2)) - 1/(2a(Q^2+aQ+a^2)). Combine the last two terms: -(2Q+a)/(6a^2) - 1/(2a) = common denominator 6a^2: -(2Q+a)/(6a^2) - (3a)/(6a^2) = -(2Q+a+3a)/(6a^2) = -(2Q+4a)/(6a^2) = -(Q+2a)/(3a^2). So derivative = 1/(3a^2(Q-a)) - (Q+2a)/(3a^2(Q^2+aQ+a^2)) = (1/(3a^2)) [1/(Q-a) - (Q+2a)/(Q^2+aQ+a^2)]. Now combine: 1/(Q-a) - (Q+2a)/(Q^2+aQ+a^2) =? Compute common denominator: (Q^2+aQ+a^2) - (Q+2a)(Q-a) over (Q-a)(Q^2+aQ+a^2). Compute (Q+2a)(Q-a)= Q^2 - aQ + 2aQ - 2a^2 = Q^2 + aQ - 2a^2. Then numerator: (Q^2+aQ+a^2) - (Q^2+aQ-2a^2) = 3a^2. So the whole becomes (1/(3a^2)) * (3a^2)/[(Q-a)(Q^2+aQ+a^2)] = 1/[(Q-a)(Q^2+aQ+a^2)] = 1/(Q^3 - a^3). Indeed Q^3-a^3 = (Q-a)(Q^2+aQ+a^2). So the derivative is correct. So the antiderivative is correct.

Thus our analytic result of 5.46 is wrong. So there must be an error in the analytic expression for H(Q). Let's re-derive H(Q) carefully.

We have: I_s = ∫ ln(Q^3-2) dQ = Q ln(Q^3-2) - 3Q - 6∫ dQ/(Q^3-2). Let J(Q) = ∫ dQ/(Q^3-2). Then I_s = [Q ln(Q^3-2) - 3Q - 6J(Q)]_{a}^{Q*}. Now we need to evaluate this at Q* and at a (limit). At Q=a, we have a singularity. So we need to compute the limit as Q→a+ of [Q ln(Q^3-2) - 3Q - 6J(Q)]. We earlier combined them into H(Q). But perhaps the combination is not H(Q) as we defined? Let's re-derive H(Q) carefully.

Let a = 2^{1/3}. Then Q^3-2 = (Q-a)(Q^2+aQ+a^2). So ln(Q^3-2) = ln|Q-a| + ln(Q^2+aQ+a^2). Then Q ln(Q^3-2) = Q ln|Q-a| + Q ln(Q^2+aQ+a^2).

Now J(Q) = ∫ dQ/(Q^3-2). Using the antiderivative: J(Q) = 1/(3a^2) ln|Q-a| - 1/(6a^2) ln(Q^2+aQ+a^2) - 1/(a^2√3) arctan((2Q+a)/(a√3)) + C. We'll take C=0.

Then -6J(Q) = -6[1/(3a^2) ln|Q-a| - 1/(6a^2) ln(Q^2+aQ+a^2) - 1/(a^2√3) arctan(...)] = - (2/a^2) ln|Q-a| + (1/a^2) ln(Q^2+aQ+a^2) + (6/(a^2√3)) arctan(...).

Now add Q ln(Q^3-2) = Q ln|Q-a| + Q ln(Q^2+aQ+a^2). So total expression inside the brackets: Q ln|Q-a| + Q ln(Q^2+aQ+a^2) - 3Q - (2/a^2) ln|Q-a| + (1/a^2) ln(Q^2+aQ+a^2) + (6/(a^2√3)) arctan(...). Combine ln|Q-a| terms: (Q - 2/a^2) ln|Q-a|. Since a^3=2, 2/a^2 = a, so Q - a. So that term becomes (Q - a) ln|Q-a|. As Q→a, (Q-a) ln(Q-a) → 0. So that term is finite and tends to 0. So we have:

I_s = [ (Q - a) ln|Q-a| + Q ln(Q^2+aQ+a^2) - 3Q + (1/a^2) ln(Q^2+aQ+a^2) + (6/(a^2√3)) arctan((2Q+a)/(a√3)) ]_{a}^{Q*}.

Notice that at Q=a, (Q-a)ln(Q-a) =0. So the term vanishes. So we can evaluate at Q=a by plugging Q=a directly into the other terms, because they are continuous. So define:

H(Q) = Q ln(Q^2+aQ+a^2) - 3Q + (1/a^2) ln(Q^2+aQ+a^2) + (6/(a^2√3)) arctan((2Q+a)/(a√3)).

Then I_s = H(Q*) - H(a), because the (Q-a)ln(Q-a) term is 0 at both ends? At Q*, (Q*-a)ln(Q*-a) is not zero, but we omitted it? Wait, we had I_s = [ (Q - a) ln|Q-a| + H(Q) ]_{a}^{Q*}. At Q*, we have (Q*-a) ln(Q*-a) as well. In our previous derivation, we included that term in H? No, we left it out. So I_s = (Q*-a) ln(Q*-a) + H(Q*) - [0 + H(a)] = (Q*-a) ln(Q*-a) + H(Q*) - H(a). Because at a, the term is 0. So we must include the (Q-a)ln(Q-a) term at Q*. Did we include that earlier? In our expression for H(Q), we did not include that term. So we need to add (Q*-a) ln(Q*-a) to H(Q*) to get the correct value. That explains the discrepancy: we omitted the (Q-a)ln(Q-a) term at the upper limit.

Q=1.1: 1/Q=0.9090909091. arctan(0.9090909) =? Since x>0.9, series converges slowly. Use identity: arctan(x) = π/2 - arctan(1/x). For x=0.90909, 1/x=1.1, so arctan(0.90909) = π/2 - arctan(1.1). That's circular. Better to compute arctan(0.90909) using known value? I can compute using the formula arctan(x) = arctan(1) - arctan((1-x)/(1+x))? For x=0.90909, (1-x)/(1+x)= (0.09091)/(1.90909)=0.04762, arctan(0.04762)=0.0476, so arctan(0.90909)=0.785398 - 0.0476 = 0.7378. That's approximate. Let's compute precisely: (1-x)/(1+x)= (1-0.9090909)/(1+0.9090909)=0.0909091/1.9090909=0.047619. arctan(0.047619)=0.047583 (since tan(0.04758)=0.04762).
